SECTION G — NOVEMBER 12, 2026 DAILY INTELLIGENCE IMPLEMENTATION PROMPTS
================================================================================

These four prompts were delivered in today's research report. They are
ASSISTANT-PROPOSED implementation opportunities based on external reference
architectures. They are not proof of existing Kymex features and must be
evaluated against the destination app before implementation.

G1. DURABLE EXECUTION JOURNAL, CRASH RECOVERY AND IDEMPOTENT AGENT OPERATIONS

Reference inspiration:
- Cloudflare Codex harness and Durable Objects work, November 9, 2026.
- Cloudflare Nanocode Durable Objects harness, November 9, 2026.
- Cloudflare Agents harness documentation.

Official references:
https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-11-09-codex-harness/
https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-11-09-nanocode-on-workers/
https://developers.cloudflare.com/agents/harnesses/

IMPLEMENTATION PROMPT:

You are the Kymex AI development/building system.

Implement a production-grade durable execution layer for autonomous Kymex
agents. The objective is to ensure long-running tasks survive process restarts,
deployment interruptions, network disconnections, worker eviction and temporary
provider failures without unnecessarily repeating completed work.

Before changing anything, inspect the existing task queue, agent execution
loops, database, persistence, retry/cancellation mechanisms, browser and coding
services, MCP execution, approval workflows, workspace storage and notifications.

Implement additively. Preserve existing task IDs, histories, data, routes,
integrations and user workflows. Do not reset or replace the existing task
system.

Create a durable execution journal. Each task and step must record:
- Task ID, execution ID, step ID and parent execution ID.
- Agent ID, workspace ID and initiating user.
- Step type, tool/provider identity and input hash.
- Execution state and timestamps.
- Output/artifact reference.
- Error class and retry count.
- Idempotency key.
- Approval/checkpoint reference.
- Recovery attempts and provenance.

Use explicit states: queued, prepared, running, completed, interrupted,
retrying, waiting for approval, failed, cancelled and reconciliation required.

Before retrying a step:
1. Inspect its journal record.
2. Determine whether it already completed.
3. Reuse persisted results where possible.
4. Check whether an external side effect may already have occurred.
5. Retry only when safe or when the provider supports idempotency.
6. Reconcile ambiguous external side effects before repeating them.

Do not promise exactly-once execution for arbitrary third-party APIs. Use
at-least-once execution with deduplication and reconciliation.

On restart, reload the journal, locate unfinished steps, recover the latest
valid checkpoint, reattach the existing task, reuse completed results, retry
safe interrupted operations and restore the correct workspace context.

Persist approval checkpoints. Bind every approval to the exact action,
parameters, target, user, workspace and expiration. Do not allow an agent to
approve its own operation.

Persist ordered events with sequence numbers and replay cursors. Reconnecting
clients must resume without duplicated events.

Integrate with workspace files, Git state and required artifacts. Use snapshots
or equivalent recovery mechanisms and document recovery-point limitations.

Extend the existing task UI with a durable execution timeline, completed and
interrupted steps, recovery activity, approvals, retry history, checkpoint,
agent progress and artifacts. Add mobile-friendly controls for resume, cancel,
safe retry and history inspection.

Integrate Telegram notifications for approval requests, recovery, permanent
failure, completion and manual reconciliation. Keep backend state authoritative.

Reuse existing entities where possible. Add only required execution run, step,
checkpoint, event, idempotency, recovery and approval-link structures.

Enforce workspace isolation, ownership checks, credential redaction, encrypted
sensitive state, audit history, execution budgets and timeouts.

Test database outages, corrupted checkpoints, missing workspaces, provider
timeouts, duplicate submissions, ambiguous side effects, expired approvals,
recovery loops, stream replay and cancellation.

Acceptance criteria:
- Tasks survive supported server restarts.
- Completed tool steps are not needlessly repeated.
- Unsafe retries pause for reconciliation.
- Approvals survive restart and remain action-bound.
- Reconnected clients replay events without duplication.
- Workspace artifacts survive supported recovery.
- Cancellation works in every valid execution state.
- No fabricated recovery or completion status is displayed.

G2. DYNAMIC WORKSPACE SKILLS AND PRIVATE DEVELOPMENT PREVIEWS

Reference inspiration:
- Cloudflare Agent Skills documentation and workspace skill integration,
  October 28–29, 2026.
- Cloudflare internal browser hostname support, October 28, 2026.

Official references:
https://developers.cloudflare.com/agents/concepts/agent-skills/
https://developers.cloudflare.com/agents/skills/sources/computer/
https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/product/browser-run/

IMPLEMENTATION PROMPT:

Implement workspace-based dynamic skills and private development previews.

Adopt the open Agent Skills directory format. Each skill has a folder, required
SKILL.md, metadata, description, optional scripts, references and assets.

Support skill sources from workspace storage, connected Git repositories and
configured cloud object storage. Do not require redeployment for every
instruction change when the architecture supports dynamic loading.

Use progressive loading:
1. Load a compact catalog.
2. Activate relevant skill instructions.
3. Load references and scripts only when required.

Validate metadata, paths, references, script types and trust classification.
Mark built-in, user-created, workspace-created, organization-approved, external
and untrusted skills distinctly.

Do not let a skill grant itself credentials, tools, network access or permissions.
Run scripts only in the existing sandbox. Require explicit approval for newly
introduced high-risk permissions.

Add a Skills workspace UI with browse, search, install, import, create, edit,
upload references, inspect scripts, enable/disable, remove, version history,
permission review and activation history.

For private development preview:
- Detect the real development server and listening port.
- Bind preview to user, workspace, project, execution and authorized browser.
- Route through a private internal hostname or secure equivalent.
- Show the rendered preview inside Kymex's browser viewer.
- Display preview URL, build status, console errors, refresh and session status.
- Permit user takeover only when actual browser control transfer is supported.
- Do not expose workspace development servers publicly by default.

Protect against SSRF, arbitrary internal network access, metadata endpoint
access, cross-tenant routing, DNS rebinding, host-header attacks and arbitrary
port forwarding. Preview routing must not become a general network tunnel.

Persist preview lifecycle, expiration and cleanup. Handle missing server, port
conflict, routing failure, expired preview and browser disconnection.

Acceptance criteria:
- Skill edits become available without redeployment where supported.
- Skills load progressively.
- Untrusted scripts cannot bypass sandbox controls.
- Coding agents can preview their actual development server.
- Preview is not public by default.
- User can inspect the rendered page.
- User takeover is real or accurately marked unavailable.
- Workspace isolation and cleanup are verified.

G3. MCP 2026-07-28 COMPATIBILITY AND STATELESS CONNECTORS

Reference:
https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28
https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-07-28-release-candidate/
https://ts.sdk.modelcontextprotocol.io/v2/migration/support-2026-07-28

IMPLEMENTATION PROMPT:

Implement compatibility with the Model Context Protocol specification
2026-07-28 while preserving existing integrations.

Inventory the current MCP client/server SDKs, transports, OAuth handling,
session persistence, tool/resource/prompt discovery, elicitation, server-
initiated requests and custom servers.

Implement protocol negotiation and modern discovery where supported. Support
modern and legacy clients during migration. Do not classify an authentication
failure as proof of legacy protocol support.

For modern stateless requests:
- Do not depend on a prior initialize request.
- Do not require a protocol-level session ID.
- Include required metadata with each request.
- Use explicit application-level handles such as task_id, browser_session_id,
  workspace_id, transaction_id and approval_id.
- Authorize every handle against the authenticated user and workspace.

Implement multi-round-trip request handling where supported. When a tool needs
additional user input, pause the operation, present the exact request, collect
the response, resume the original operation and return the actual result.

Support modern routing metadata and validate header/body consistency.
Implement cache hints only when supported and ensure cache scope respects user,
tenant, server and permission boundaries.

Maintain legacy compatibility until individual integrations are verified.
Treat Tasks, Skills and MCP Apps as optional extensions, not universal core
capabilities.

Review OAuth authorization, issuer/audience/resource binding, secure token
storage, least-privilege scopes, origin validation, SSRF protection and audit.

Update the integrations dashboard with protocol revision, transport,
capabilities, extensions, authentication, scopes, last connection, warnings
and migration status.

Test modern-to-modern, modern-to-legacy, legacy compatibility, authentication
failure, discovery timeout, multi-round-trip continuation, cross-tenant handle
access, cache isolation and unsupported extensions.

Do not remove or silently downgrade existing connectors.

IMPLEMENTATION PROMPT:

context limits, pricing, tool support, multimodal support, availability,
rate limits, regions and configuration state.

Introduce roles:
- Architect: designs the solution and boundaries.

Add Gemini 4 Carbon as an optional provider only after verifying the actual

Route by task difficulty, capability, context, latency, budget, user preference

Use isolated workspaces or branches for concurrent coding agents. Give each

SECTION H — CROSS-CUTTING MIGRATION RULES FOR THE DESTINATION PROJECT
================================================================================

H1. NON-DESTRUCTIVE IMPLEMENTATION

Every change must be additive or migration-safe. Before modifying existing
routes, entities, functions, components, jobs, integrations or user records,
inspect dependencies and create a checkpoint.

Never:
- Replace the destination app with a new generic chatbot.
- Remove existing pages to make room for new features.
- Delete or reset user data.
- Duplicate existing working features.
- overwrite secrets or connection records.
- seed fake production data.
- fabricate provider health, tool results or browser activity.
- mark a task complete before backend verification.
- expose credentials to client code or model context.

H2. REQUIREMENT TRACEABILITY

Create an internal migration matrix with:
- Requirement ID.
- Source archive/section.
- Destination route/component.
- Backend function/API.
- Entity/schema.
- Provider/integration dependency.
- Permission/approval requirement.
- Current status.
- Implementation status.
- Test evidence.
- Remaining blocker.

Statuses:
- Existing and verified.
- Existing but unverified.
- Partially implemented.
- Missing.
- Provider/credential blocked.
- Unsupported by current platform.
- Superseded.
- Requires owner decision.

H3. FRONTEND QUALITY

Every new surface must include:
- Loading state.
- Empty state.
- Error state.
- Permission-denied state.
- Offline/disconnected state where relevant.
- Success state based on confirmed backend result.
- Responsive mobile and desktop behavior.
- Keyboard accessibility.
- Screen-reader labels.
- Reduced-motion behavior.
- Touch-friendly controls.

H4. BACKEND QUALITY

Every mutation must validate:
- Authenticated actor.
- Ownership.
- Workspace/project scope.
- Role and permissions.
- Tool/provider authorization.
- Approval requirements.
- Rate and budget limits.
- Idempotency where appropriate.

Every asynchronous job must have stable identifiers, lifecycle events,
cancellation/resume behavior where supported, normalized errors, audit events
and truthful final state.

H5. SECRET AND CREDENTIAL HANDLING

Use server-side secret storage. Never place API keys, OAuth refresh tokens,
passwords, one-time codes or private credentials in:
- Chat messages.
- Model prompts.
- Client bundles.
- Browser local storage.
- Source code.
- Logs.
- Public files.
- Shared workspaces without explicit authorization.

Use references to secrets in the UI, not their raw values. Support rotation,
revocation and least-privilege scopes.

H6. PROVIDER CAPABILITY REGISTRY

Provider features must be capability-driven. Distinguish:
- Native Kymex capability.
- Connected provider capability.
- Beta/preview capability.
- Unsupported capability.
- User-led handoff.

Do not advertise a capability simply because a provider's marketing page
describes it. Confirm that the official API or authorized integration exposes
the needed operation.

H7. TESTING AND RELEASE

Run unit, integration, security, migration, concurrency, recovery and end-to-end
tests. Verify mobile flows. Test provider failure and credential revocation.

Deliver a final report listing:
- Features already present before migration.
- New features actually implemented.
- Existing features modified.
- Files/routes/entities/functions changed.
- Migrations performed.
- Integrations connected and tested.
- Required credentials/approvals.
- Test commands and results.
- Known limitations.
- Rollback instructions.
- Work still blocked.

A prompt being included in this archive is not implementation evidence.

You are now implementing the Kymex AI migration on:

Do not implement on:
https://the-kymex-ai.base44.app

The historical source project is a reference only.

First audit the destination application and produce a complete inventory.
Then reconcile every requirement in this archive against actual code, routes,
entities, backend functions, integrations and runtime behavior.

Preserve every existing working feature and all user data. Do not create a
parallel implementation where an existing capability already satisfies the
requirement. Do not treat historical prompts as proof of current functionality.

Implement in dependency-aware vertical slices:
1. Backup, audit and requirement mapping.
2. Core data and permission contracts.
3. Persistent tasks, event history and recovery.
4. Browser sessions and user takeover.
5. MCP and connector registry.
6. Skills and workspace previews.
7. Model routing and specialist agents.
8. Telegram command center.
9. Chat rich-link rendering.
10. Creative studio.
11. Admin controls, observability and cost management.
12. Security, regression and mobile acceptance.

The exact sequence may be adjusted based on real dependencies, but no existing
feature may be removed to simplify implementation.

If the Base44 platform cannot provide a required capability natively, identify
the required external service or infrastructure, its official API, credentials,
cost and limitations. Do not fake the behavior.

Do not stop after creating UI. Complete the backend wiring, persistence,
permissions, provider calls, error states and acceptance tests.

Do not declare the migration complete until the destination app itself has
passed the acceptance tests and the owner receives a truthful implementation
report.
